[

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.4, F32.5, F32.8, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.4, F33.8, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because something has been scaring me a little, and I wasn’t sure if I should wait and see if it passes or if I needed to talk to someone. I don’t want to hurt myself, I really don’t, but lately I’ve had these thoughts like… if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. And even saying that out loud makes me feel awful, because it sounds more dramatic than I mean it to, but it’s been coming up enough that I thought, no, I probably shouldn’t just keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up tomorrow?\n\nPatient: It’s been recent enough that it’s what finally pushed me to make the appointment. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, I still sort of go through the motions with the things I usually like. I’ll set out my paints at the table, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because normally that’s something that makes me feel like myself. But then when I’m actually there, it’s like… I just sit and stare at the materials. The paint, the brushes, the clay, whatever is in front of me.

And I keep waiting for that little spark to show up, you know, that feeling of wanting to start or getting absorbed in it, but it just doesn’t come. It’s not that I hate it now or anything. It’s more like it’s all still there, but I can’t seem to connect to it the way I used to.

I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed some big, obvious change, like suddenly sleeping all day or being up the whole night. It’s more that my sleep feels a little… unsettled, maybe? But I couldn’t tell you exactly how much is different versus how much I’m just paying attention to it now because everything else feels off.

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, not in any clear way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything, and I haven’t stepped on the scale and thought, oh wow, something’s really changed. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think I do. More than I used to, anyway. It’s not usually about big things, which almost makes it feel sillier when I say it out loud. It’s these tiny things at work that I keep going over and over in my head after the fact.

Like, if I notice one of the display signs was crooked for part of the day, or I explain something to a trainee and they still look confused, I’ll just… latch onto it. Instead of thinking, okay, that happens, I start telling myself I’m not doing my job right, that I’m a useless supervisor, that everyone else must be noticing how poorly I’m handling things.

And logically, I know a crooked sign is not exactly a disaster. I’ve supervised people for a long time, so I do know that. But lately those little mistakes don’t feel little in my head. They sit there, and I keep replaying them like evidence that I’m failing somehow.

I can’t say I marked the exact first day, because at first it was more like this passing thought that I would try to dismiss, like, oh, I’m just tired or overwhelmed. But then it started coming back in that same form — not that I want to hurt myself, because I really don’t — but more like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier.\n\nAnd that scared me. Not because I have some plan or anything like that, but because it felt like a thought I shouldn’t just ignore anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you usually like to do lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still sort of go through the motions with the things I usually like. I’ll set out my paints at the table, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because normally that’s something that makes me feel like myself. But then when I’m actually there, it’s like… I just sit and stare at the materials. The paint, the brushes, the clay, whatever is in front of me.\n\nAnd I keep waiting for that little spark to show up, you know, that feeling of wanting to start or getting absorbed in it, but it just doesn’t come. It’s not that I hate it now or anything. It’s more like it’s all still there, but I can’t seem to connect to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed some big, obvious change, like suddenly sleeping all day or being up the whole night. It’s more that my sleep feels a little… unsettled, maybe? But I couldn’t tell you exactly how much is different versus how much I’m just paying attention to it now because everything else feels off.\n\nI still go to bed, I still get up, and I haven’t been tracking the hours or anything. Some nights I lie there thinking about whether I’m going to feel the same in the morning, and that probably doesn’t help. But if you asked me for a clear number, like “I used to sleep this many hours and now I sleep this many,” I don’t really have that. I just know it hasn’t felt especially restful lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any clear way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything, and I haven’t stepped on the scale and thought, oh wow, something’s really changed. I suppose there could be a few pounds here or there, because that happens normally, but nothing that I would call significant.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping close track, though. I know some people weigh themselves regularly, but I don’t unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. So I can’t give you an exact number. But just from how I feel day to day, I haven’t noticed any major weight change.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. More than I used to, anyway. It’s not usually about big things, which almost makes it feel sillier when I say it out loud. It’s these tiny things at work that I keep going over and over in my head after the fact.\n\nLike, if I notice one of the display signs was crooked for part of the day, or I explain something to a trainee and they still look confused, I’ll just… latch onto it. Instead of thinking, okay, that happens, I start telling myself I’m not doing my job right, that I’m a useless supervisor, that everyone else must be noticing how poorly I’m handling things.\n\nAnd logically, I know a crooked sign is not exactly a disaster. I’ve supervised people for a long time, so I do know that. But lately those little mistakes don’t feel little in my head. They sit there, and I keep replaying them like evidence that I’m failing somehow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes, actually… though it’s not consistent, which is part of why I wasn’t sure whether to mention it. Some days I feel sort of keyed up in my body, like I’ll be sitting in the living room and then realize I’ve been pacing back and forth for no real reason. Or I’ll look down and notice I’ve been picking at my cuticles without even really being aware I was doing it. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.

And then other days it’s almost the opposite. At work, just walking from the break room back out to the sales floor can feel strangely slow, like I’m moving through something heavier than usual. I don’t know if other people would notice it from the outside, but I notice it in myself. It’s like my body can’t decide whether it wants to be restless or sluggish.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I have noticed that, especially with my art things. I used to really look forward to painting, or going to pottery class. It wasn’t just the activity, it was the whole feeling of it — setting everything out, choosing colors, getting my hands into the clay, having that little bit of excitement about what I might make.

Lately I’ll still try to do the motions of it. I’ll put my paints out on the table, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to go to pottery because I know it’s good for me and I used to love it. But then when I’m actually there, I just sort of… stare at the materials. The brushes, the paint, the clay, whatever it is. And there’s no spark. It feels like I’m looking at someone else’s hobby, if that makes sense.

I don’t necessarily hate it, exactly. It’s not that I suddenly dislike painting or pottery. It’s more like the pleasure part has gone quiet. I keep expecting that once I start, I’ll get absorbed in it the way I used to, but a lot of the time I’m just sitting there, kind of blank, wondering why I can’t feel interested in something that used to mean so much to me.

I suppose there could be a few pounds here or there, because that happens normally, but nothing that I would call significant.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping close track, though. I know some people weigh themselves regularly, but I don’t unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. So I can’t give you an exact number. But just from how I feel day to day, I haven’t noticed any major weight change.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. More than I used to, anyway. It’s not usually about big things, which almost makes it feel sillier when I say it out loud. It’s these tiny things at work that I keep going over and over in my head after the fact.\n\nLike, if I notice one of the display signs was crooked for part of the day, or I explain something to a trainee and they still look confused, I’ll just… latch onto it. Instead of thinking, okay, that happens, I start telling myself I’m not doing my job right, that I’m a useless supervisor, that everyone else must be noticing how poorly I’m handling things.\n\nAnd logically, I know a crooked sign is not exactly a disaster. I’ve supervised people for a long time, so I do know that. But lately those little mistakes don’t feel little in my head. They sit there, and I keep replaying them like evidence that I’m failing somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually… though it’s not consistent, which is part of why I wasn’t sure whether to mention it. Some days I feel sort of keyed up in my body, like I’ll be sitting in the living room and then realize I’ve been pacing back and forth for no real reason. Or I’ll look down and notice I’ve been picking at my cuticles without even really being aware I was doing it. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nAnd then other days it’s almost the opposite. At work, just walking from the break room back out to the sales floor can feel strangely slow, like I’m moving through something heavier than usual. I don’t know if other people would notice it from the outside, but I notice it in myself. It’s like my body can’t decide whether it wants to be restless or sluggish.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but now you don't find pleasure in them anymore?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have noticed that, especially with my art things. I used to really look forward to painting, or going to pottery class. It wasn’t just the activity, it was the whole feeling of it — setting everything out, choosing colors, getting my hands into the clay, having that little bit of excitement about what I might make.\n\nLately I’ll still try to do the motions of it. I’ll put my paints out on the table, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to go to pottery because I know it’s good for me and I used to love it. But then when I’m actually there, I just sort of… stare at the materials. The brushes, the paint, the clay, whatever it is. And there’s no spark. It feels like I’m looking at someone else’s hobby, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t necessarily hate it, exactly. It’s not that I suddenly dislike painting or pottery. It’s more like the pleasure part has gone quiet. I keep expecting that once I start, I’ll get absorbed in it the way I used to, but a lot of the time I’m just sitting there, kind of blank, wondering why I can’t feel interested in something that used to mean so much to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do, though I don’t know that I would have used the word “burden” right away. It’s more like I get this feeling that I’m failing people, especially at work, and then it turns into this really harsh judgment of myself.

I’ll make some tiny mistake — or not even necessarily a real mistake, just something that didn’t go perfectly — like a display sign being crooked, or one of the trainees looking confused after I explained something. And then I’ll keep replaying it later, over and over, like I’m building a whole case against myself. I start thinking, “You’re supposed to be the supervisor. You should have caught that. You should have explained it better.” And then it kind of spirals into, “Maybe I’m not good at this anymore. Maybe I’m useless in this role.”

It sounds excessive when I say it, because I know on one level those are normal things that happen during a workday. But in the moment it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like proof that I’m letting people down.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.4, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because something has been scaring me a little, and I wasn’t sure if I should wait and see if it passes or if I needed to talk to someone. I don’t want to hurt myself, I really don’t, but lately I’ve had these thoughts like… if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. And even saying that out loud makes me feel awful, because it sounds more dramatic than I mean it to, but it’s been coming up enough that I thought, no, I probably shouldn’t just keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up tomorrow?\n\nPatient: It’s been recent enough that it’s what finally pushed me to make the appointment. I can’t say I marked the exact first day, because at first it was more like this passing thought that I would try to dismiss, like, oh, I’m just tired or overwhelmed. But then it started coming back in that same form — not that I want to hurt myself, because I really don’t — but more like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier.\n\nAnd that scared me. Not because I have some plan or anything like that, but because it felt like a thought I shouldn’t just ignore anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you usually like to do lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still sort of go through the motions with the things I usually like. I’ll set out my paints at the table, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because normally that’s something that makes me feel like myself. But then when I’m actually there, it’s like… I just sit and stare at the materials. The paint, the brushes, the clay, whatever is in front of me.\n\nAnd I keep waiting for that little spark to show up, you know, that feeling of wanting to start or getting absorbed in it, but it just doesn’t come. It’s not that I hate it now or anything. It’s more like it’s all still there, but I can’t seem to connect to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed some big, obvious change, like suddenly sleeping all day or being up the whole night. It’s more that my sleep feels a little… unsettled, maybe? But I couldn’t tell you exactly how much is different versus how much I’m just paying attention to it now because everything else feels off.\n\nI still go to bed, I still get up, and I haven’t been tracking the hours or anything. Some nights I lie there thinking about whether I’m going to feel the same in the morning, and that probably doesn’t help. But if you asked me for a clear number, like “I used to sleep this many hours and now I sleep this many,” I don’t really have that. I just know it hasn’t felt especially restful lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any clear way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything, and I haven’t stepped on the scale and thought, oh wow, something’s really changed. I suppose there could be a few pounds here or there, because that happens normally, but nothing that I would call significant.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping close track, though. I know some people weigh themselves regularly, but I don’t unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. So I can’t give you an exact number. But just from how I feel day to day, I haven’t noticed any major weight change.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. More than I used to, anyway. It’s not usually about big things, which almost makes it feel sillier when I say it out loud. It’s these tiny things at work that I keep going over and over in my head after the fact.\n\nLike, if I notice one of the display signs was crooked for part of the day, or I explain something to a trainee and they still look confused, I’ll just… latch onto it. Instead of thinking, okay, that happens, I start telling myself I’m not doing my job right, that I’m a useless supervisor, that everyone else must be noticing how poorly I’m handling things.\n\nAnd logically, I know a crooked sign is not exactly a disaster. I’ve supervised people for a long time, so I do know that. But lately those little mistakes don’t feel little in my head. They sit there, and I keep replaying them like evidence that I’m failing somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually… though it’s not consistent, which is part of why I wasn’t sure whether to mention it. Some days I feel sort of keyed up in my body, like I’ll be sitting in the living room and then realize I’ve been pacing back and forth for no real reason. Or I’ll look down and notice I’ve been picking at my cuticles without even really being aware I was doing it. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nAnd then other days it’s almost the opposite. At work, just walking from the break room back out to the sales floor can feel strangely slow, like I’m moving through something heavier than usual. I don’t know if other people would notice it from the outside, but I notice it in myself. It’s like my body can’t decide whether it wants to be restless or sluggish.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but now you don't find pleasure in them anymore?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have noticed that, especially with my art things. I used to really look forward to painting, or going to pottery class. It wasn’t just the activity, it was the whole feeling of it — setting everything out, choosing colors, getting my hands into the clay, having that little bit of excitement about what I might make.\n\nLately I’ll still try to do the motions of it. I’ll put my paints out on the table, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to go to pottery because I know it’s good for me and I used to love it. But then when I’m actually there, I just sort of… stare at the materials. The brushes, the paint, the clay, whatever it is. And there’s no spark. It feels like I’m looking at someone else’s hobby, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t necessarily hate it, exactly. It’s not that I suddenly dislike painting or pottery. It’s more like the pleasure part has gone quiet. I keep expecting that once I start, I’ll get absorbed in it the way I used to, but a lot of the time I’m just sitting there, kind of blank, wondering why I can’t feel interested in something that used to mean so much to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t know that I would have used the word “burden” right away. It’s more like I get this feeling that I’m failing people, especially at work, and then it turns into this really harsh judgment of myself.\n\nI’ll make some tiny mistake — or not even necessarily a real mistake, just something that didn’t go perfectly — like a display sign being crooked, or one of the trainees looking confused after I explained something. And then I’ll keep replaying it later, over and over, like I’m building a whole case against myself. I start thinking, “You’re supposed to be the supervisor. You should have caught that. You should have explained it better.” And then it kind of spirals into, “Maybe I’m not good at this anymore. Maybe I’m useless in this role.”\n\nIt sounds excessive when I say it, because I know on one level those are normal things that happen during a workday. But in the moment it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like proof that I’m letting people down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I keep expecting that once I start, I’ll get absorbed in it the way I used to, but a lot of the time I’m just sitting there, kind of blank, wondering why I can’t feel interested in something that used to mean so much to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t know that I would have used the word “burden” right away. It’s more like I get this feeling that I’m failing people, especially at work, and then it turns into this really harsh judgment of myself.\n\nI’ll make some tiny mistake — or not even necessarily a real mistake, just something that didn’t go perfectly — like a display sign being crooked, or one of the trainees looking confused after I explained something. And then I’ll keep replaying it later, over and over, like I’m building a whole case against myself. I start thinking, “You’re supposed to be the supervisor. You should have caught that. You should have explained it better.” And then it kind of spirals into, “Maybe I’m not good at this anymore. Maybe I’m useless in this role.”\n\nIt sounds excessive when I say it, because I know on one level those are normal things that happen during a workday. But in the moment it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like proof that I’m letting people down.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow many weeks have you been feeling this way?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 62 / first_line_supervisor_of_retail_sales_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This whole up-and-down stretch with my mood and energy has been going on for about two months, since the trouble with the new district manager really started.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d say it’s been about two months now… maybe not every single day in exactly the same way, but this whole up-and-down stretch with my mood and energy has been going on since the trouble with the new district manager really started.

I remember it because that situation at work sort of became the marker for it. Before that I had stressful days, of course, but this particular feeling — the way it’s been affecting me and kind of lingering — I’d put it at around eight weeks, give or take.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because something has been scaring me a little, and I wasn’t sure if I should wait and see if it passes or if I needed to talk to someone. I don’t want to hurt myself, I really don’t, but lately I’ve had these thoughts like… if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. And even saying that out loud makes me feel awful, because it sounds more dramatic than I mean it to, but it’s been coming up enough that I thought, no, I probably shouldn’t just keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up tomorrow?\n\nPatient: It’s been recent enough that it’s what finally pushed me to make the appointment. I can’t say I marked the exact first day, because at first it was more like this passing thought that I would try to dismiss, like, oh, I’m just tired or overwhelmed. But then it started coming back in that same form — not that I want to hurt myself, because I really don’t — but more like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier.\n\nAnd that scared me. Not because I have some plan or anything like that, but because it felt like a thought I shouldn’t just ignore anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you usually like to do lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still sort of go through the motions with the things I usually like. I’ll set out my paints at the table, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because normally that’s something that makes me feel like myself. But then when I’m actually there, it’s like… I just sit and stare at the materials. The paint, the brushes, the clay, whatever is in front of me.\n\nAnd I keep waiting for that little spark to show up, you know, that feeling of wanting to start or getting absorbed in it, but it just doesn’t come. It’s not that I hate it now or anything. It’s more like it’s all still there, but I can’t seem to connect to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed some big, obvious change, like suddenly sleeping all day or being up the whole night. It’s more that my sleep feels a little… unsettled, maybe? But I couldn’t tell you exactly how much is different versus how much I’m just paying attention to it now because everything else feels off.\n\nI still go to bed, I still get up, and I haven’t been tracking the hours or anything. Some nights I lie there thinking about whether I’m going to feel the same in the morning, and that probably doesn’t help. But if you asked me for a clear number, like “I used to sleep this many hours and now I sleep this many,” I don’t really have that. I just know it hasn’t felt especially restful lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any clear way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything, and I haven’t stepped on the scale and thought, oh wow, something’s really changed. I suppose there could be a few pounds here or there, because that happens normally, but nothing that I would call significant.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping close track, though. I know some people weigh themselves regularly, but I don’t unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. So I can’t give you an exact number. But just from how I feel day to day, I haven’t noticed any major weight change.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. More than I used to, anyway. It’s not usually about big things, which almost makes it feel sillier when I say it out loud. It’s these tiny things at work that I keep going over and over in my head after the fact.\n\nLike, if I notice one of the display signs was crooked for part of the day, or I explain something to a trainee and they still look confused, I’ll just… latch onto it. Instead of thinking, okay, that happens, I start telling myself I’m not doing my job right, that I’m a useless supervisor, that everyone else must be noticing how poorly I’m handling things.\n\nAnd logically, I know a crooked sign is not exactly a disaster. I’ve supervised people for a long time, so I do know that. But lately those little mistakes don’t feel little in my head. They sit there, and I keep replaying them like evidence that I’m failing somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually… though it’s not consistent, which is part of why I wasn’t sure whether to mention it. Some days I feel sort of keyed up in my body, like I’ll be sitting in the living room and then realize I’ve been pacing back and forth for no real reason. Or I’ll look down and notice I’ve been picking at my cuticles without even really being aware I was doing it. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nAnd then other days it’s almost the opposite. At work, just walking from the break room back out to the sales floor can feel strangely slow, like I’m moving through something heavier than usual. I don’t know if other people would notice it from the outside, but I notice it in myself. It’s like my body can’t decide whether it wants to be restless or sluggish.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but now you don't find pleasure in them anymore?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have noticed that, especially with my art things. I used to really look forward to painting, or going to pottery class. It wasn’t just the activity, it was the whole feeling of it — setting everything out, choosing colors, getting my hands into the clay, having that little bit of excitement about what I might make.\n\nLately I’ll still try to do the motions of it. I’ll put my paints out on the table, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to go to pottery because I know it’s good for me and I used to love it. But then when I’m actually there, I just sort of… stare at the materials. The brushes, the paint, the clay, whatever it is. And there’s no spark. It feels like I’m looking at someone else’s hobby, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t necessarily hate it, exactly. It’s not that I suddenly dislike painting or pottery. It’s more like the pleasure part has gone quiet. I keep expecting that once I start, I’ll get absorbed in it the way I used to, but a lot of the time I’m just sitting there, kind of blank, wondering why I can’t feel interested in something that used to mean so much to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t know that I would have used the word “burden” right away. It’s more like I get this feeling that I’m failing people, especially at work, and then it turns into this really harsh judgment of myself.\n\nI’ll make some tiny mistake — or not even necessarily a real mistake, just something that didn’t go perfectly — like a display sign being crooked, or one of the trainees looking confused after I explained something. And then I’ll keep replaying it later, over and over, like I’m building a whole case against myself. I start thinking, “You’re supposed to be the supervisor. You should have caught that. You should have explained it better.” And then it kind of spirals into, “Maybe I’m not good at this anymore. Maybe I’m useless in this role.”\n\nIt sounds excessive when I say it, because I know on one level those are normal things that happen during a workday. But in the moment it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like proof that I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months now… maybe not every single day in exactly the same way, but this whole up-and-down stretch with my mood and energy has been going on since the trouble with the new district manager really started.\n\nI remember it because that situation at work sort of became the marker for it. I keep expecting that once I start, I’ll get absorbed in it the way I used to, but a lot of the time I’m just sitting there, kind of blank, wondering why I can’t feel interested in something that used to mean so much to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t know that I would have used the word “burden” right away. It’s more like I get this feeling that I’m failing people, especially at work, and then it turns into this really harsh judgment of myself.\n\nI’ll make some tiny mistake — or not even necessarily a real mistake, just something that didn’t go perfectly — like a display sign being crooked, or one of the trainees looking confused after I explained something. And then I’ll keep replaying it later, over and over, like I’m building a whole case against myself. I start thinking, “You’re supposed to be the supervisor. You should have caught that. You should have explained it better.” And then it kind of spirals into, “Maybe I’m not good at this anymore. Maybe I’m useless in this role.”\n\nIt sounds excessive when I say it, because I know on one level those are normal things that happen during a workday. But in the moment it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like proof that I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months now… maybe not every single day in exactly the same way, but this whole up-and-down stretch with my mood and energy has been going on since the trouble with the new district manager really started.\n\nI remember it because that situation at work sort of became the marker for it. Before that I had stressful days, of course, but this particular feeling — the way it’s been affecting me and kind of lingering — I’d put it at around eight weeks, give or take.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any thoughts or heard any voices that aren't your own?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

I don’t want to hurt myself, I really don’t, but lately I’ve had these thoughts like… if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. And even saying that out loud makes me feel awful, because it sounds more dramatic than I mean it to, but it’s been coming up enough that I thought, no, I probably shouldn’t just keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up tomorrow?\n\nPatient: It’s been recent enough that it’s what finally pushed me to make the appointment. I can’t say I marked the exact first day, because at first it was more like this passing thought that I would try to dismiss, like, oh, I’m just tired or overwhelmed. But then it started coming back in that same form — not that I want to hurt myself, because I really don’t — but more like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier.\n\nAnd that scared me. Not because I have some plan or anything like that, but because it felt like a thought I shouldn’t just ignore anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you usually like to do lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still sort of go through the motions with the things I usually like. I’ll set out my paints at the table, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because normally that’s something that makes me feel like myself. But then when I’m actually there, it’s like… I just sit and stare at the materials. The paint, the brushes, the clay, whatever is in front of me.\n\nAnd I keep waiting for that little spark to show up, you know, that feeling of wanting to start or getting absorbed in it, but it just doesn’t come. It’s not that I hate it now or anything. It’s more like it’s all still there, but I can’t seem to connect to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed some big, obvious change, like suddenly sleeping all day or being up the whole night. It’s more that my sleep feels a little… unsettled, maybe? But I couldn’t tell you exactly how much is different versus how much I’m just paying attention to it now because everything else feels off.\n\nI still go to bed, I still get up, and I haven’t been tracking the hours or anything. Some nights I lie there thinking about whether I’m going to feel the same in the morning, and that probably doesn’t help. But if you asked me for a clear number, like “I used to sleep this many hours and now I sleep this many,” I don’t really have that. I just know it hasn’t felt especially restful lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any clear way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything, and I haven’t stepped on the scale and thought, oh wow, something’s really changed. I suppose there could be a few pounds here or there, because that happens normally, but nothing that I would call significant.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping close track, though. I know some people weigh themselves regularly, but I don’t unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. So I can’t give you an exact number. But just from how I feel day to day, I haven’t noticed any major weight change.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. Instead of thinking, okay, that happens, I start telling myself I’m not doing my job right, that I’m a useless supervisor, that everyone else must be noticing how poorly I’m handling things.\n\nAnd logically, I know a crooked sign is not exactly a disaster. I don’t know if other people would notice it from the outside, but I notice it in myself. It’s like my body can’t decide whether it wants to be restless or sluggish.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but now you don't find pleasure in them anymore?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have noticed that, especially with my art things. I used to really look forward to painting, or going to pottery class. It wasn’t just the activity, it was the whole feeling of it — setting everything out, choosing colors, getting my hands into the clay, having that little bit of excitement about what I might make.\n\nLately I’ll still try to do the motions of it. I’ll put my paints out on the table, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to go to pottery because I know it’s good for me and I used to love it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I think I have. More than two weeks, definitely. The more I think about it, the low, flat part has really been there for about six months now.

It’s not always dramatic, exactly. It’s more like things that used to help me feel like myself just stopped doing that. I used to take morning walks and feel at least a little clearer afterward, like I’d sort of reset my day. And I used to enjoy my art classes — not that I was amazing at them, but they were good for me, they gave me something to look forward to. Lately, though, they haven’t felt restorative in the same way. I’ll go through the motions or think about going, but it doesn’t lift me much.

So yes, if you’re asking whether there’s been a stretch of at least two weeks where I felt depressed or lost interest, I’d say it’s been much longer than that — around six months of this dull, low feeling hanging around.

And even saying that out loud makes me feel awful, because it sounds more dramatic than I mean it to, but it’s been coming up enough that I thought, no, I probably shouldn’t just keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up tomorrow?\n\nPatient: It’s been recent enough that it’s what finally pushed me to make the appointment. I can’t say I marked the exact first day, because at first it was more like this passing thought that I would try to dismiss, like, oh, I’m just tired or overwhelmed. Not because I have some plan or anything like that, but because it felt like a thought I shouldn’t just ignore anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you usually like to do lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still sort of go through the motions with the things I usually like. I’ll set out my paints at the table, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because normally that’s something that makes me feel like myself. But then when I’m actually there, it’s like… I just sit and stare at the materials. The paint, the brushes, the clay, whatever is in front of me.\n\nAnd I keep waiting for that little spark to show up, you know, that feeling of wanting to start or getting absorbed in it, but it just doesn’t come. It’s not that I hate it now or anything. It’s more like it’s all still there, but I can’t seem to connect to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed some big, obvious change, like suddenly sleeping all day or being up the whole night. It’s more that my sleep feels a little… unsettled, maybe? But I couldn’t tell you exactly how much is different versus how much I’m just paying attention to it now because everything else feels off.\n\nI still go to bed, I still get up, and I haven’t been tracking the hours or anything. Some nights I lie there thinking about whether I’m going to feel the same in the morning, and that probably doesn’t help. But if you asked me for a clear number, like “I used to sleep this many hours and now I sleep this many,” I don’t really have that. I just know it hasn’t felt especially restful lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any clear way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything, and I haven’t stepped on the scale and thought, oh wow, something’s really changed. I suppose there could be a few pounds here or there, because that happens normally, but nothing that I would call significant.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping close track, though. I know some people weigh themselves regularly, but I don’t unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. So I can’t give you an exact number. But just from how I feel day to day, I haven’t noticed any major weight change.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. More than I used to, anyway. It’s not usually about big things, which almost makes it feel sillier when I say it out loud. It’s these tiny things at work that I keep going over and over in my head after the fact.\n\nLike, if I notice one of the display signs was crooked for part of the day, or I explain something to a trainee and they still look confused, I’ll just… latch onto it. Instead of thinking, okay, that happens, I start telling myself I’m not doing my job right, that I’m a useless supervisor, that everyone else must be noticing how poorly I’m handling things.\n\nAnd logically, I know a crooked sign is not exactly a disaster. I’ve supervised people for a long time, so I do know that. But lately those little mistakes don’t feel little in my head. They sit there, and I keep replaying them like evidence that I’m failing somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually… though it’s not consistent, which is part of why I wasn’t sure whether to mention it. Some days I feel sort of keyed up in my body, like I’ll be sitting in the living room and then realize I’ve been pacing back and forth for no real reason. Or I’ll look down and notice I’ve been picking at my cuticles without even really being aware I was doing it. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nAnd then other days it’s almost the opposite. At work, just walking from the break room back out to the sales floor can feel strangely slow, like I’m moving through something heavier than usual. I don’t know if other people would notice it from the outside, but I notice it in myself. It’s like my body can’t decide whether it wants to be restless or sluggish.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but now you don't find pleasure in them anymore?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have noticed that, especially with my art things. I used to really look forward to painting, or going to pottery class. It wasn’t just the activity, it was the whole feeling of it — setting everything out, choosing colors, getting my hands into the clay, having that little bit of excitement about what I might make.\n\nLately I’ll still try to do the motions of it. I’ll put my paints out on the table, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to go to pottery because I know it’s good for me and I used to love it. But then when I’m actually there, I just sort of… stare at the materials. The brushes, the paint, the clay, whatever it is. And there’s no spark. It feels like I’m looking at someone else’s hobby, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t necessarily hate it, exactly. It’s not that I suddenly dislike painting or pottery. It’s more like the pleasure part has gone quiet. I keep expecting that once I start, I’ll get absorbed in it the way I used to, but a lot of the time I’m just sitting there, kind of blank, wondering why I can’t feel interested in something that used to mean so much to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t know that I would have used the word “burden” right away. It’s more like I get this feeling that I’m failing people, especially at work, and then it turns into this really harsh judgment of myself.\n\nI’ll make some tiny mistake — or not even necessarily a real mistake, just something that didn’t go perfectly — like a display sign being crooked, or one of the trainees looking confused after I explained something. You should have explained it better.” And then it kind of spirals into, “Maybe I’m not good at this anymore. Maybe I’m useless in this role.”\n\nIt sounds excessive when I say it, because I know on one level those are normal things that happen during a workday. But in the moment it doesn’t feel normal. Before that I had stressful days, of course, but this particular feeling — the way it’s been affecting me and kind of lingering — I’d put it at around eight weeks, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts or heard any voices that aren't your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not like someone else is putting them there.\n\nAnd I haven’t heard voices, like a voice talking to me when no one is there. I don’t think I’ve had anything like that. Instead of thinking, okay, that happens, I start telling myself I’m not doing my job right, that I’m a useless supervisor, that everyone else must be noticing how poorly I’m handling things.\n\nAnd logically, I know a crooked sign is not exactly a disaster. I’ve supervised people for a long time, so I do know that. But lately those little mistakes don’t feel little in my head. They sit there, and I keep replaying them like evidence that I’m failing somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually… though it’s not consistent, which is part of why I wasn’t sure whether to mention it. Some days I feel sort of keyed up in my body, like I’ll be sitting in the living room and then realize I’ve been pacing back and forth for no real reason. Or I’ll look down and notice I’ve been picking at my cuticles without even really being aware I was doing it. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nAnd then other days it’s almost the opposite. At work, just walking from the break room back out to the sales floor can feel strangely slow, like I’m moving through something heavier than usual. I don’t know if other people would notice it from the outside, but I notice it in myself. It’s like my body can’t decide whether it wants to be restless or sluggish.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but now you don't find pleasure in them anymore?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have noticed that, especially with my art things. I used to really look forward to painting, or going to pottery class. It wasn’t just the activity, it was the whole feeling of it — setting everything out, choosing colors, getting my hands into the clay, having that little bit of excitement about what I might make.\n\nLately I’ll still try to do the motions of it. I’ll put my paints out on the table, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to go to pottery because I know it’s good for me and I used to love it. But then when I’m actually there, I just sort of… stare at the materials. The brushes, the paint, the clay, whatever it is. And there’s no spark. It feels like I’m looking at someone else’s hobby, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t necessarily hate it, exactly. It’s not that I suddenly dislike painting or pottery. It’s more like the pleasure part has gone quiet. I keep expecting that once I start, I’ll get absorbed in it the way I used to, but a lot of the time I’m just sitting there, kind of blank, wondering why I can’t feel interested in something that used to mean so much to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t know that I would have used the word “burden” right away. It’s more like I get this feeling that I’m failing people, especially at work, and then it turns into this really harsh judgment of myself.\n\nI’ll make some tiny mistake — or not even necessarily a real mistake, just something that didn’t go perfectly — like a display sign being crooked, or one of the trainees looking confused after I explained something. And then I’ll keep replaying it later, over and over, like I’m building a whole case against myself. I start thinking, “You’re supposed to be the supervisor. You should have caught that. You should have explained it better.” And then it kind of spirals into, “Maybe I’m not good at this anymore. Maybe I’m useless in this role.”\n\nIt sounds excessive when I say it, because I know on one level those are normal things that happen during a workday. But in the moment it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like proof that I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months now… maybe not every single day in exactly the same way, but this whole up-and-down stretch with my mood and energy has been going on since the trouble with the new district manager really started.\n\nI remember it because that situation at work sort of became the marker for it. Before that I had stressful days, of course, but this particular feeling — the way it’s been affecting me and kind of lingering — I’d put it at around eight weeks, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts or heard any voices that aren't your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean… I have thoughts that feel very harsh or repetitive sometimes, but they still feel like they’re coming from me, if that makes sense. Not like someone else is putting them there.\n\nAnd I haven’t heard voices, like a voice talking to me when no one is there. I don’t think I’ve had anything like that. Sometimes I second-guess myself or get caught up in my own head, but it doesn’t feel like an outside voice or something separate from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in most activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. More than two weeks, definitely. The more I think about it, the low, flat part has really been there for about six months now.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, exactly. It’s more like things that used to help me feel like myself just stopped doing that. I used to take morning walks and feel at least a little clearer afterward, like I’d sort of reset my day. And I used to enjoy my art classes — not that I was amazing at them, but they were good for me, they gave me something to look forward to. Lately, though, they haven’t felt restorative in the same way. I’ll go through the motions or think about going, but it doesn’t lift me much.\n\nSo yes, if you’re asking whether there’s been a stretch of at least two weeks where I felt depressed or lost interest, I’d say it’s been much longer than that — around six months of this dull, low feeling hanging around.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Completely unable? No, I wouldn’t say that. I mean, I’m still getting up, I’m still going to work, I’m still showering and feeding myself and all of that. So from the outside I probably look like I’m functioning, maybe just quieter than usual.

But it’s definitely affected how I do things. At the store, I’ve started avoiding extra conversations with staff if I don’t absolutely have to have them. Like if someone comes up with something small or wants my opinion, I’ll sometimes keep it very brief or put it off, because I just don’t feel like I have the energy or the confidence to handle it the way I normally would.

And simple decisions — things I used to just make quickly, like how to handle a display or a scheduling question or some small store issue — I find myself delaying them. I’ll think, “I’ll decide in an hour,” or “Let me look at it again tomorrow,” even when it’s not that complicated. It’s not that I can’t understand what needs to be done, exactly. It’s more that I don’t trust my own judgment as much, so I hesitate and second-guess myself.

So no, I haven’t been unable to take care of myself, but I’m not operating like I usually do. It feels like I’m doing the minimum to keep everything going, and anything extra feels harder than it should.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because something has been scaring me a little, and I wasn’t sure if I should wait and see if it passes or if I needed to talk to someone. I don’t want to hurt myself, I really don’t, but lately I’ve had these thoughts like… if I just didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier. And even saying that out loud makes me feel awful, because it sounds more dramatic than I mean it to, but it’s been coming up enough that I thought, no, I probably shouldn’t just keep brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up tomorrow?\n\nPatient: It’s been recent enough that it’s what finally pushed me to make the appointment. I can’t say I marked the exact first day, because at first it was more like this passing thought that I would try to dismiss, like, oh, I’m just tired or overwhelmed. But then it started coming back in that same form — not that I want to hurt myself, because I really don’t — but more like, if I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that would be easier.\n\nAnd that scared me. Not because I have some plan or anything like that, but because it felt like a thought I shouldn’t just ignore anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you usually like to do lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I still sort of go through the motions with the things I usually like. I’ll set out my paints at the table, or I’ll tell myself I should go to pottery class because normally that’s something that makes me feel like myself. But then when I’m actually there, it’s like… I just sit and stare at the materials. The paint, the brushes, the clay, whatever is in front of me.\n\nAnd I keep waiting for that little spark to show up, you know, that feeling of wanting to start or getting absorbed in it, but it just doesn’t come. It’s not that I hate it now or anything. It’s more like it’s all still there, but I can’t seem to connect to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed some big, obvious change, like suddenly sleeping all day or being up the whole night. It’s more that my sleep feels a little… unsettled, maybe? But I couldn’t tell you exactly how much is different versus how much I’m just paying attention to it now because everything else feels off.\n\nI still go to bed, I still get up, and I haven’t been tracking the hours or anything. Some nights I lie there thinking about whether I’m going to feel the same in the morning, and that probably doesn’t help. But if you asked me for a clear number, like “I used to sleep this many hours and now I sleep this many,” I don’t really have that. I just know it hasn’t felt especially restful lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any clear way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything, and I haven’t stepped on the scale and thought, oh wow, something’s really changed. I suppose there could be a few pounds here or there, because that happens normally, but nothing that I would call significant.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping close track, though. I know some people weigh themselves regularly, but I don’t unless I’m at a doctor’s office or something. So I can’t give you an exact number. But just from how I feel day to day, I haven’t noticed any major weight change.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do. More than I used to, anyway. It’s not usually about big things, which almost makes it feel sillier when I say it out loud. It’s these tiny things at work that I keep going over and over in my head after the fact.\n\nLike, if I notice one of the display signs was crooked for part of the day, or I explain something to a trainee and they still look confused, I’ll just… latch onto it. Instead of thinking, okay, that happens, I start telling myself I’m not doing my job right, that I’m a useless supervisor, that everyone else must be noticing how poorly I’m handling things.\n\nAnd logically, I know a crooked sign is not exactly a disaster. I’ve supervised people for a long time, so I do know that. But lately those little mistakes don’t feel little in my head. They sit there, and I keep replaying them like evidence that I’m failing somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually… though it’s not consistent, which is part of why I wasn’t sure whether to mention it. Some days I feel sort of keyed up in my body, like I’ll be sitting in the living room and then realize I’ve been pacing back and forth for no real reason. Or I’ll look down and notice I’ve been picking at my cuticles without even really being aware I was doing it. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nAnd then other days it’s almost the opposite. At work, just walking from the break room back out to the sales floor can feel strangely slow, like I’m moving through something heavier than usual. I don’t know if other people would notice it from the outside, but I notice it in myself. It’s like my body can’t decide whether it wants to be restless or sluggish.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that you used to enjoy doing, but now you don't find pleasure in them anymore?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have noticed that, especially with my art things. I used to really look forward to painting, or going to pottery class. It wasn’t just the activity, it was the whole feeling of it — setting everything out, choosing colors, getting my hands into the clay, having that little bit of excitement about what I might make.\n\nLately I’ll still try to do the motions of it. I’ll put my paints out on the table, or I’ll tell myself I’m going to go to pottery because I know it’s good for me and I used to love it. But then when I’m actually there, I just sort of… stare at the materials. The brushes, the paint, the clay, whatever it is. And there’s no spark. It feels like I’m looking at someone else’s hobby, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t necessarily hate it, exactly. It’s not that I suddenly dislike painting or pottery. It’s more like the pleasure part has gone quiet. I keep expecting that once I start, I’ll get absorbed in it the way I used to, but a lot of the time I’m just sitting there, kind of blank, wondering why I can’t feel interested in something that used to mean so much to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t know that I would have used the word “burden” right away. It’s more like I get this feeling that I’m failing people, especially at work, and then it turns into this really harsh judgment of myself.\n\nI’ll make some tiny mistake — or not even necessarily a real mistake, just something that didn’t go perfectly — like a display sign being crooked, or one of the trainees looking confused after I explained something. And then I’ll keep replaying it later, over and over, like I’m building a whole case against myself. I start thinking, “You’re supposed to be the supervisor. You should have caught that. You should have explained it better.” And then it kind of spirals into, “Maybe I’m not good at this anymore. Maybe I’m useless in this role.”\n\nIt sounds excessive when I say it, because I know on one level those are normal things that happen during a workday. But in the moment it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like proof that I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months now… maybe not every single day in exactly the same way, but this whole up-and-down stretch with my mood and energy has been going on since the trouble with the new district manager really started.\n\nI remember it because that situation at work sort of became the marker for it. Before that I had stressful days, of course, but this particular feeling — the way it’s been affecting me and kind of lingering — I’d put it at around eight weeks, give or take.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts or heard any voices that aren't your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean… I have thoughts that feel very harsh or repetitive sometimes, but they still feel like they’re coming from me, if that makes sense. Not like someone else is putting them there.\n\nAnd I haven’t heard voices, like a voice talking to me when no one is there. I don’t think I’ve had anything like that. Sometimes I second-guess myself or get caught up in my own head, but it doesn’t feel like an outside voice or something separate from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in most activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. More than two weeks, definitely. The more I think about it, the low, flat part has really been there for about six months now.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, exactly. It’s more like things that used to help me feel like myself just stopped doing that. I used to take morning walks and feel at least a little clearer afterward, like I’d sort of reset my day. And I used to enjoy my art classes — not that I was amazing at them, but they were good for me, they gave me something to look forward to. Lately, though, they haven’t felt restorative in the same way. I’ll go through the motions or think about going, but it doesn’t lift me much.\n\nSo yes, if you’re asking whether there’s been a stretch of at least two weeks where I felt depressed or lost interest, I’d say it’s been much longer than that — around six months of this dull, low feeling hanging around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your depression was significantly worse than it is now?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say there was one clear period where it was dramatically worse than now… it’s more that lately most mornings start out the same way. I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like the color has already drained out of the day before I even get dressed for the store.\n\nSome mornings are heavier than others, I suppose, but I’m not sure I can point to a separate time and say, “That was the worst spell.” It’s been more of this persistent morning heaviness that sets the tone before the day has even begun.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt completely unable to take care of yourself or perform daily tasks due to your depression?\n\nPatient: Completely unable? No, I wouldn’t say that. I mean, I’m still getting up, I’m still going to work, I’m still showering and feeding myself and all of that. So from the outside I probably look like I’m functioning, maybe just quieter than usual.\n\nBut it’s definitely affected how I do things. At the store, I’ve started avoiding extra conversations with staff if I don’t absolutely have to have them. Like if someone comes up with something small or wants my opinion, I’ll sometimes keep it very brief or put it off, because I just don’t feel like I have the energy or the confidence to handle it the way I normally would.\n\nAnd simple decisions — things I used to just make quickly, like how to handle a display or a scheduling question or some small store issue — I find myself delaying them. I’ll think, “I’ll decide in an hour,” or “Let me look at it again tomorrow,” even when it’s not that complicated. It’s not that I can’t understand what needs to be done, exactly. It’s more that I don’t trust my own judgment as much, so I hesitate and second-guess myself.\n\nSo no, I haven’t been unable to take care of myself, but I’m not operating like I usually do. It feels like I’m doing the minimum to keep everything going, and anything extra feels harder than it should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. manifestation: This whole up-and-down stretch with my mood and energy has been going on for about two months, since the trouble with the new district manager really started.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 6 months\n  manifestation: The low, flat part has been present for about six months now, long enough that my usual routines like art classes and morning walks stopped feeling restorative.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 62 / first_line_supervisor_of_retail_sales_worker / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S032\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in most activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. More than two weeks, definitely. The more I think about it, the low, flat part has really been there for about six months now.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, exactly. I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like the color has already drained out of the day before I even get dressed for the store.\n\nSome mornings are heavier than others, I suppose, but I’m not sure I can point to a separate time and say, “That was the worst spell.” It’s been more of this persistent morning heaviness that sets the tone before the day has even begun.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt completely unable to take care of yourself or perform daily tasks due to your depression?\n\nPatient: Completely unable? No, I wouldn’t say that. I mean, I’m still getting up, I’m still going to work, I’m still showering and feeding myself and all of that. At the store, I’ve started avoiding extra conversations with staff if I don’t absolutely have to have them. Like if someone comes up with something small or wants my opinion, I’ll sometimes keep it very brief or put it off, because I just don’t feel like I have the energy or the confidence to handle it the way I normally would.\n\nAnd simple decisions — things I used to just make quickly, like how to handle a display or a scheduling question or some small store issue — I find myself delaying them. I’ll think, “I’ll decide in an hour,” or “Let me look at it again tomorrow,” even when it’s not that complicated. It’s not that I can’t understand what needs to be done, exactly. It’s more that I don’t trust my own judgment as much, so I hesitate and second-guess myself.\n\nSo no, I haven’t been unable to take care of myself, but I’m not operating like I usually do. It feels like I’m doing the minimum to keep everything going, and anything extra feels harder than it should.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not much, honestly. I keep trying with the things that used to give me pleasure, because part of me thinks maybe if I just show up, it’ll come back.

Like with painting — I’ll set out my paints at home, get the brushes ready, maybe even put everything where the light is nice. And then I’ll just sit there looking at it. I know what I used to feel, that little pull to start, that spark where one color would make me think of another. But now it’s like I’m staring at someone else’s supplies.

And pottery class is similar. I’ll think, “Okay, go, it’ll be good for you,” and sometimes I do go. But once I’m there, I just look at the clay and the tools and everyone else getting started, and I don’t feel that old enjoyment. I’m present, technically, but it doesn’t reach me the way it used to.

So I suppose I still do the activities, or at least attempt them, but the pleasure part… that’s mostly missing.
